
## ideation
**Core difficulty:** We need the shortest walk from i to j whose concatenated edge labels form a palindrome. A naïve BFS over all possible strings is impossible. The trick is to use a product graph: state `(u, v)` means the current built string is a palindrome that starts at `i`, ends at `j`, and we have walked from the left end to `u` and from the right end to `v`. Expanding by one character on the left (edge from `u` to `u'`) and one on the right (edge from `v` to `v'`) with the same label keeps the palindrome property. The middle of the string is handled by transitions where both sides take the same next character (two edges per step, length increases by 2). Distances on this product graph correspond to palindrome length.

**Candidate approaches:**
1. BFS on product graph of size N² (states `(u, v)`) with edges:
   - Left step only (odd length palindrome building from middle): `u -> u'`, same label, `v` stays.
   - Right step only: `v -> v'`, same label, `u` stays.
   - Pair step (even expansion from both sides): `u -> u'` and `v -> v'` with same label, length +2.
2. 0‑1 BFS is not needed because all transitions have equal cost 1 (or 2 for the pair step—can treat as two unit steps).
3. Since we need all-pairs shortest palindrome path lengths, we can run a BFS starting from every pair `(s, t)` simultaneously in a multi-source BFS on the product graph, with a visited array for each source, but simpler: run a separate BFS for each ordered pair `(i, j)` — N² BFS each O(N²) = O(N⁴) = 10⁸ for N=100, might be borderline but okay in Python with careful implementation. Better: run a single BFS per source vertex i in the original graph to compute distances to all pairs (u, v) on the product graph where the two sides are synchronised; but the product graph is still N².

A cleaner standard solution: For each start vertex `s`, do a BFS on states `(u, v)` where we simultaneously walk forward from `s` and backward from every possible `t`. **Standard approach (recap):** 
We construct a graph whose vertices are ordered pairs `(u, v)` of original vertices. For each `u` and `v`, and for each letter `c`:
- If there are edges `u -> u'` and `v -> v'` both labeled `c`, we add a directed edge from `(u, v)` to `(u', v')` (or undirected, doesn't matter). This represents matching a character on the left and on the right.
- Additionally, for each `u` and each edge `u -> u'` labeled `c`, we add a directed edge from `(u, u)` to `(u', u)` and from `(u, u)` to `(u, u')`. This represents taking one step from the center.

But actually, we can avoid the special center case by observing that the transition `(u, u) -> (u', u')` with the same label `c` on both sides corresponds to adding two characters `cc` to the palindrome (length +2). To get odd-length palindromes, we need to add a single character at the center. This can be achieved by starting from `(u, u)` and making a transition where one side moves and the other stays: e.g., `(u, u) -> (u', u)` with label `c`. This adds one character. Then subsequent "both sides" transitions add two characters each. So the distance in the product graph (with unit cost for each transition) equals the number of characters added. The empty string corresponds to `(i, i)` with distance 0. So the BFS distance from `(i, j)` in this product graph is exactly the answer for `(i, j)`.

2. For any `u` and any edge `u -> u'` with label `c`: add edge `(u, u) -> (u', u)` with cost 1, and also `(u, u) -> (u, u')` with cost 1. (This handles the center character.)

Let's just derive the algorithm from scratch. We need for all pairs (i, j) the shortest palindrome walk length. N ≤ 100, so N^2 = 10,000. We can run a BFS for each source i in the product graph of size N^2, and the answer for (i, j) is the distance to some accepting state. The product graph is built as follows:

Let the state be (u, v). The meaning: we are building a palindrome. The left part of the palindrome is formed by the walk from i to u (reading the edge labels in order). The right part is formed by the walk from j to v (reading the edge labels in order, but this part will be reversed and appended to the left part). The total string so far is L + reverse(R), where L is the sequence of labels on the path from i to u, and R is the sequence of labels on the path from j to v. We want the final string to be a palindrome, which means L + reverse(R) is a palindrome. This is equivalent to saying that the sequence L concatenated with the reverse of R is a palindrome. A string is a palindrome iff it equals its reverse. So L + reverse(R) = reverse(L + reverse(R)) = reverse(reverse(R)) + reverse(L) = R + reverse(L). So we need L + reverse(R) = R + reverse(L). This is a condition on L and R.

We can maintain the invariant that L and R are such that the palindrome property holds so far. Initially, L and R are empty, so the state is (i, j) and the string is empty (palindrome). To add a character to the left, we take an edge u -> u' with label c. Then L becomes L + c. The new total string is (L + c) + reverse(R). For this to be a palindrome, we need that c is the first character of the total string, so it must match the last character of the total string. The last character of the total string is the last character of reverse(R), which is the last character of R. So if R is non-empty, its last character must be c. If R is empty, then the total string is just L + c, which must be a palindrome. So L + c must be a palindrome, meaning the first character of L must be c, and so on. This is complicated.

Better: We can think of the walk from i to j as a sequence of edges. The palindrome condition means the first edge label equals the last, second equals second-last, etc. So we can match the first and last edges, then the second and second-last, etc. When we match the first edge (i -> i1, label c) and the last edge (j1 -> j, label c), we remove them and the remaining problem is to find a palindrome walk from i1 to j1. So the state is (u, v) meaning the remaining walk is from u to v. Initially, (i, j). We want to reduce this to a walk of length 0 (which is a palindrome) or length 1 (which is a palindrome if the single edge exists). So the BFS state is (u, v) and we want the shortest path to any state where the walk from u to v is a palindrome of length 0 or 1. The walk from u to v of length 0 means u == v. The walk of length 1 means there is an edge from u to v with any label. So the accepting states are: (k, k) for any k, and (u, v) where there is an edge from u to v. But we can also consider that the empty walk (length 0) is a palindrome, so (k, k) is accepting. The walk of length 1 is a palindrome, so (u, v) with an edge is also accepting. No, the remaining walk must itself be a palindrome for the total walk to be a palindrome. Since the matched parts are already symmetric, the remaining walk must be a palindrome. So the accepting states are exactly those (u, v) such that there is a palindrome walk from u to v. That's recursive.

However, if we start at (i, j) and we only take transitions that consume edges from the original graph, the total number of edges consumed in the original graph is 2 * (number of both-sides transitions) + (number of one-sided transitions). The BFS distance in the product graph (with weights 1 for one-sided and 2 for both-sides) exactly equals the total number of edges consumed. So if we can find the shortest path from (i, j) to any state (u, v) where the remaining walk is a palindrome, that distance is the total number of edges consumed, which is the palindrome length. **The correct algorithm (from memory of similar problems):**
We will build a graph with N^2 vertices, but the edges are built differently. For each pair (u, v), we will consider the "distance" in the product graph where we can traverse edges that match the first and last characters. The standard solution is to use BFS on the graph where vertices are pairs (u, v), and edges are:
- For each letter c, if there is an edge u -> u' with label c and an edge v -> v' with label c, we add a directed edge from (u, v) to (u', v'). (This is matching the first of the left with the first of the right. But as discussed, this builds the left walk forward and the right walk forward. The total string is left + right. For it to be a palindrome, left must be the reverse of right. So matching first of left with first of right is not correct; we need first of left with last of right. So the right side should move backwards. So the edge should be from (u, v) to (u', v') if u->u' (c) and v'->v (c). This is the correct matching for the first and last edges. So we use v'->v.)
- Additionally, for each edge u -> u' with label c, we add a directed edge from (u, u) to (u', u). (This is for the center character when the two pointers meet.)
- Then, we want the shortest path from (i, j) to any state (k, k) where the remaining walk is empty? But as we saw, reaching (k, k) does not mean the remaining walk is empty; it means the pointers are at the same vertex. The remaining walk is a walk from k to k. To finish, we need that walk to be a palindrome. But if we start from (i, j) and only use the above transitions, the total number of edges consumed in the original graph is exactly the BFS distance (with weight 1 for center moves, weight 2 for matching moves? No, the BFS distance counts the number of transitions. Each matching transition consumes 2 edges (one from left, one from right). Each center transition consumes 1 edge (from left, and the right side doesn't move? Actually, the center transition from (u, u) to (u', u) consumes one edge from the left, and the right side stays at u. But the right side is a walk from u to u. So the remaining walk after the center transition is from u' to u. That is a walk of length 1. So the total string is the matched part plus this remaining walk. The BFS distance in the product graph (with unit weight per transition) is not the palindrome length; it's the number of transitions. We need to assign weight 2 to matching transitions and weight 1 to center transitions. So we can run a 0-1-2 BFS, or we can transform the graph to have only unit weights by splitting the matching transition into two unit steps with an intermediate state that remembers the expected character.

Given N ≤ 100, we can afford 26 * N^2 states. So the simplest correct solution is to use a state of (u, v, k) where k is the expected character (0 for none). The number of states is 26 * 100^2 = 260,000. BFS on this graph is O(26 * N^2 * 26) = O(26^2 N^2) = 6,760,000, which is fine.

Let's re-read the problem: "Among all (not necessarily simple) paths from vertex i to vertex j whose concatenation of labels on the edges forms a palindrome, what is the length of the shortest such path?"
So the walk must start at i and end at j. The product graph approach usually works by building the walk from the middle outwards. That is, we start at some middle vertex m, and build the walk by adding characters to the left and right. The final walk is from i to j. The state is (u, v) where u is the left end and v is the right end. We start at (m, m) and expand to (i, j). The distance from (m, m) to (i, j) in the expansion graph is the length. The reverse graph: from (u, v) to (u', v') if u'->u and v'->v with the same label. That is, we can add a character to the left and right by taking edges backwards. Also, from (u, u) we can add a single character by taking an edge backwards. Then the distance from (m, m) to (i, j) is the length. This is equivalent to the forward BFS from (i, j) to (m, m) in the original product graph. The original product graph (forward) has edges: from (u, v) to (u', v') if u->u' and v->v' with the same label. And from (u, u) to (u', u) if u->u'. The target is any (m, m). No, if we define the transition as adding one character to the left and one to the right simultaneously, that's 2 characters per transition. But if we use the state machine with expected character, each transition adds 1 character. The state machine with expected character is the correct one that gives the length directly. Let's formalize that.

**State machine for palindrome length:**
We want the shortest walk from i to j. We can think of building the walk from the middle. The middle is some vertex m. The walk is formed by taking steps left and right from m. The state is (l, r) where l is the current left vertex, r is the current right vertex. Initially, l = m, r = m. We want to reach l = i, r = j. The total length is the number of steps. At each step, we can add one character to the left and one to the right (if l != r) or just one character to the left (if l == r). But we must ensure the characters match for the palindrome. So the step from (l, r) is:
- If l != r: we must take an edge l -> l' with label c and an edge r' -> r with label c. Then we go to (l', r'). This adds 2 characters. But we want to add 1 character at a time. So we can break it into: from (l, r), take an edge l -> l' with label c, and go to (l', r) with the condition that the next step from r must be with label c. So we need an intermediate state.
- So the state is (l, r, k) where k is the expected character for the right side (0 if none). The number of states is 26 * N^2.
Transitions:
- If k == 0: we can take an edge l -> l' with label c. Then we go to (l', r, c). This means we added a character on the left, and now we need to add the same character on the right.
- If k == c: we must take an edge r -> r' with label c. Then we go to (l, r', 0). This adds the matching character on the right.
- Additionally, we need to handle the case where the walk ends. The walk is complete when l == i and r == j and k == 0. Also, we can have the walk be a single character: if l == r and k == 0, we can take an edge l -> l' with label c, and if l' == j? No, we want to end at i and j. So the target state is (i, j, 0). The reverse graph: from (l, r, c) to (l', r, 0) if l' -> l with label c. From (l, r, 0) to (l, r', c) if r' -> r with label c. And also we can start at any (m, m, 0) with distance 0. The answer for (i, j) is the distance to (i, j, 0) from the set of start states. Since we want the shortest walk, we take the minimum over m of the distance from (m, m, 0) to (i, j, 0). Actually, the standard solution is to run a BFS on the graph with N^2 states and edges as follows:
- For each u, v, and for each letter c, if there is an edge u -> u' with label c and an edge v -> v' with label c, add an edge from (u, v) to (u', v') with cost 1. (This is matching the first of the left with the first of the right, but in the reverse direction? Let's not worry about direction; we can define the product graph to be undirected or directed appropriately. The key is that the BFS distance in this graph, when doubled, gives the palindrome length. And we also have base cases for single characters.)

Given the time, I will implement the BFS on the state space (l, r, k) as I had for the reverse graph, but correctly this time. The reverse graph from the middle outward is the most robust. Let's re-derive the reverse graph carefully.

We want to find the shortest walk from i to j that is a palindrome. We can think of the walk as being built from the middle outwards. The middle is some vertex m. The walk has a left part from i to m, and a right part from m to j. The labels on the left part (in order) must equal the reverse of the labels on the right part. So if we list the labels on the left part as L, and on the right part as R, we need L = reverse(R). This means the first label of L equals the last label of R, etc. So we can build the walk by starting at the middle and adding pairs of characters to the left and right. At each step, we add one character to the left and one to the right. The state is the current left vertex l, the current right vertex r. Initially, l = m, r = m. We want to reach l = i, r = j. The transition: from (l, r), we can add a character c to the left and a character c to the right. This means we take an edge from l to l' with label c, and an edge from r' to r with label c. (Because the right part is built from the middle outwards, so the new right character is an edge from r' to r.) So the new state is (l', r'). The cost is 1 (for one pair of characters). The total length is 2 * (number of steps). Additionally, we can add a single character at the center. This happens when l == r. From (m, m), we can add one character to the left (or right) and finish. That is, we take an edge from m to m' with label c, and we want the new left to be i and the new right to be j. If we take a left step, the new state is (m', m). The remaining right part is from m to j. We need the right part to be a palindrome? Actually, the total walk is the left part (m->m') plus the right part (m->j). For the total string to be a palindrome, the right part must be the reverse of the left part. The left part is just "c". So the right part must be "c". So the right part must start with an edge from m to somewhere with label c, and then end at j. But in the reverse expansion, we are building both parts simultaneously. So we can just continue the BFS. The center is naturally handled by the state machine: when l == r, we can take a step that only moves l or only moves r. But we must ensure the characters match. So we can allow a transition from (m, m) to (m', m) with the condition that the next step on the right must match. This is exactly the state with expected character.

So the correct state machine for building from the middle is:
- States: (l, r, k) where k is 0..25 (0 = no expected char, 1..26 = expected char).
- Transitions (forward, i.e., from the middle to the outside):
  - If k == 0:
    - We can take a step on the left: for each edge l -> l' with label c, go to (l', r, c) with cost 1.
    - We can also take a step on the right: for each edge r -> r' with label c, go to (l, r', c) with cost 1.
    - But wait, we need the characters to match. If we take a left step, we must later take a right step with the same label. If we take a right step, we must later take a left step with the same label. So we can just use one of them? Actually, we can always choose to expand left first, then right, or vice versa. The state machine with a single expected character works: when k=0, we can take a left or right step. When k=c, we must take a step on the other side with label c.
  - If k == c:
    - We must take a step on the side that hasn't moved. If we took a left step last, we must take a right step. So if we are in (l, r, c) after a left step, we must take a right step: for each edge r -> r' with label c, go to (l, r', 0) with cost 1.
- The target is (i, j, 0). The sources are all (m, m, 0) for m=1..N.
- Additionally, we need to allow the case where the walk is a single edge. That is, from (i, j, 0) we can go to (j, j, 0) with cost 1 if there is an edge i->j. In the reverse BFS (from sources to target), this corresponds to an edge from (j, j, 0) to (i, j, 0) with cost 1. So we add that edge to the reverse graph.
- The reverse graph (which we run BFS on, from sources to all states) has edges:
  - From (l, r, 0) to (l', r, c) if l' -> l (c). (Reverse of left step)
  - From (l, r, c) to (l, r', 0) if r' -> r (c). (Reverse of right step)
  - From (l, l, 0) to (l', l, 0) if l' -> l (c). (Reverse of finishing a single edge? Actually, from (j, j, 0) we want to go to (i, j, 0) if i->j. So this edge is exactly that: from (l, l, 0) to (l', l, 0) if l' -> l. This covers the single edge case.)
- Also, we need to allow the center expansion in the forward direction: from (m, m, 0) we can take a left step to (m', m, c) and then a right step to (m', m', 0). This is covered by the first two rules. The distance from (m, m, 0) to (m', m', 0) will be 2. The total length of the walk is the distance in this state machine. Let's test on sample 1.

So the correct reverse BFS is:
- States: (l, r, k) with k=0..25.
- Sources: all (m, m, 0) for m=0..N-1. Dist 0.
- Transitions from (l, r, 0):
  - For each edge l' -> l (c): (l', r, c) with cost 1.
  - For each edge r' -> r (c): (l, r', c) with cost 1.
- Transitions from (l, r, c):
  - For each edge l' -> l (c): (l', r, 0) with cost 1. (Wait, this is the reverse of the forward right step? Let's be systematic.)
The forward graph (building from middle):
- (l, r, 0) -> (l', r, c) if l -> l' (c) [left step]
- (l, r, 0) -> (l, r', c) if r -> r' (c) [right step]
- (l, r, c) -> (l, r', 0) if r -> r' (c) [match right step]
- (l, r, c) -> (l', r, 0) if l -> l' (c) [match left step]  (This is symmetric: if we took a right step, we must match with a left step.)
Actually, we can just enforce that the expected character is always the character of the last step. So if we took a left step with c, we are in (l', r, c) and must take a right step with c. If we took a right step with c, we are in (l, r', c) and must take a left step with c. So the forward transitions are:
- (l, r, 0) -> (l', r, c) if l -> l' (c)  [left step, expect c on right]
- (l, r, 0) -> (l, r', c) if r -> r' (c)  [right step, expect c on left]
- (l, r, c) -> (l, r', 0) if r -> r' (c)  [match right step, was left step]
- (l, r, c) -> (l', r, 0) if l -> l' (c)  [match left step, was right step]
- Also, the single edge case: from (l, r, 0) we can go to (r, r, 0) if l -> r (c) and we want to finish. This is a transition that takes a left step and immediately finishes because the right is already at the target? Actually, the target is (i, j, 0). So from (i, j, 0) we can go to (j, j, 0) with cost 1 if i->j. So in the forward graph, (i, j, 0) -> (j, j, 0) is an edge if i->j. In the reverse graph, (j, j, 0) -> (i, j, 0) is an edge if i->j.
- Also, the center: from (m, m, 0) we can take a left step and then a right step to get to (m', m', 0). That's covered by the above.

The reverse graph (for BFS from sources):
- (l', r, c) -> (l, r, 0) if l -> l' (c)  [reverse of left step]
- (l, r', c) -> (l, r, 0) if r -> r' (c)  [reverse of right step]
- (l, r', 0) -> (l, r, c) if r -> r' (c)  [reverse of match right step]
- (l', r, 0) -> (l, r, c) if l -> l' (c)  [reverse of match left step]
- (j, j, 0) -> (i, j, 0) if i -> j (c)  [single edge]

- (u, v) -> (u', v') cost 2 if u -> u' (c) and v' -> v (c).
- (u, u) -> (u', u) cost 1 if u -> u' (c). (Center)
- (u, v) -> (v, v) cost 1 if u -> v (c). (Finish single edge)
- (u, v) -> (u, u) cost 1 if v -> u (c). (Finish single edge reverse)

But BFS on 270,000 nodes with 14 million edges is fine in C++, but in Python it might be borderline. However, N=100, so N^2=10,000. 27 * 10,000 = 270,000. This is the Dijkstra on the product graph. Since N is small, we can run Dijkstra from each (i, j) to all (m, m) on this graph. The graph has N^2 nodes. The edges: for each (u, v), for each letter c, if there is an edge u -> u' (c) and an edge v' -> v (c), add edge (u, v) -> (u', v') with weight 2. Also, from (u, u) add edge to (u', u) with weight 1 if u -> u' (c). Also, from (u, v) add edge to (v, v) with weight 1 if u -> v (c) and also (u, u) if v -> u (c). This graph has up to 7 million edges. Running Dijkstra from each (i, j) is N^2 * (E log V) = 10000 * (7M * 14) = 10^9, too slow. Observation: The product graph edges can be generated on the fly. For each (u, v) we want to relax its neighbors. For a given (u, v), the outgoing edges are:
- For each u' such that u -> u' (c), and each v' such that v' -> v (c) with the same c: (u', v') with weight 2.
- If u == v: for each u' such that u -> u' (c): (u', u) with weight 1.
- If there is an edge u -> v (c): (v, v) with weight 1.
- If there is an edge v -> u (c): (u, u) with weight 1.
We can run a multi-source BFS using a deque (since weights are 1 and 2). We start with all (m, m) in the deque with distance 0. For each state popped, we relax its neighbors. This is O(V * (out_u * in_v + out_u)) which is O(N^2 * (26^2 + 26)) = O(10000 * 702) = 7 million operations per state? No, for each state we process its outgoing edges. The total number of edge relaxations is the total number of edges in the product graph, which is sum over all (u, v) of (out_u * in_v). This is at most N^2 * 26 * 26 = 6.76 million. Plus the weight 1 edges. So total relaxations ~ 7 million. Running a 0-1-2 BFS (using a deque for weight 1, and a second deque for weight 2) is O(V + E) = O(10^4 + 7M) = 7 million. This is very fast! We just need to be careful with the deque. For 0-1 BFS, we can use weight 1 and 2. The standard 0-1 BFS uses a deque where weight 0 edges are pushed to the front, weight 1 to the back. For weight 2, we can push to the back, but we need to ensure the distance is correct. Alternatively, we can run Dijkstra with a binary heap. 7 million edges with binary heap: each edge relaxation is a push to the heap. That's 7 million pushes and pops, each O(log V) = O(log 10000) ~ 14. So 7M * 14 = 98M operations. In Python, this might be a bit slow but possibly okay if optimized with heapq. Or we can just use a simple BFS with a deque and handle weight 2 by doing a "2-step BFS": we can just run Dijkstra with a heap; 7 million heap operations in Python might be around 1-2 seconds, which is acceptable. But we need to be careful with the graph size. We can store the product graph implicitly.

Let's design the implicit graph BFS:
- dist array of size N x N, initialized to infinity.
- For all m, dist[m][m] = 0. Use a deque.
- While deque not empty:
  - Pop (u, v).
  - If u == v:
    - For each edge u -> u' (c): if dist[u'][u] > dist[u][u] + 1: update and push to front? Actually, weight 1 edges: we can push to the front of the deque to process them before weight 2 edges. But we have weight 2 edges as well. We can use a deque for 0-1-2 BFS: push weight 1 to front, weight 2 to back. But we also have weight 0? No.
  - For weight 2 edges: for each edge u -> u' (c) and each edge v' -> v (c) with same c: if dist[u'][v'] > dist[u][v] + 2: update and push to back.
This is a standard 0-1-2 BFS. The deque will process nodes in order of increasing distance. Since the maximum distance is bounded (at most 26 * N * something), it's fine.
We need to efficiently find, for a given (u, v) and a letter c, the pairs (u', v') such that u->u' (c) and v'->v (c). We can precompute for each vertex v and each letter c: the list of incoming edges to v with label c. And for each vertex u and letter c: the list of outgoing edges from u with label c. Then for a given (u, v), we iterate over c from 0 to 25, and for each u' in out[u][c] and v' in in[v][c], we relax (u', v') with weight 2. The number of such pairs is out[u][c] * in[v][c]. The total over all (u, v) and c is sum_{u,v,c} out[u][c] * in[v][c] = (sum_{u,c} out[u][c]) * (sum_{v,c} in[v][c])? Not exactly, but it's at most N * 26 * 26 * N = N^2 * 26^2 = 6.76 million. So this is efficient.
We also need to handle the weight 1 edges: from (u, u) to (u', u) for each u->u' (c). And from (u, v) to (v, v) if u->v (c) and from (u, v) to (u, u) if v->u (c). We can handle these as part of the relaxation.

Let's write the algorithm:
- Precompute out_edges[u] = list of (v, c) for each u.
- Precompute in_edges[v] = list of (u, c) for each v.
- Initialize dist[N][N] = INF.
- For m in 0..N-1: dist[m][m] = 0. Add to deque.
- While deque:
  - pop (u, v)
  - d = dist[u][v]
  - If u == v:
    - For each (u', c) in out_edges[u]:
      - if dist[u'][u] > d + 1:
        - dist[u'][u] = d + 1
        - appendleft (u', u)  # weight 1
  - For each c in 0..25:
    - For each u' in out_edges[u][c]:
      - For each v' in in_edges[v][c]:
        - if dist[u'][v'] > d + 2:
          - dist[u'][v'] = d + 2
          - append (u', v')  # weight 2
  - Also, handle the finishing edges: if there is an edge u -> v (c), then (v, v) can be reached with cost 1. So:
    - if dist[v][v] > d + 1: update and appendleft.
  - And if there is an edge v -> u (c), then (u, u) can be reached with cost 1. Update similarly.
This will compute the shortest distance from any (m, m) to all (i, j). The answer for (i, j) is dist[i][j].

- From (l, r, 0):

  - For each edge r -> v (c): (l, v, c) with cost 1. (Right step: add character to right, expect on left)
- From (l, r, c):
  - For each edge r -> v (c): (u, v, 0) with cost 1. (Match right step)
  - For each edge u -> l (c): (u, v, 0) with cost 1. (Match left step)
- Target: (i, j, 0). The distance is the number of characters.

So the state machine BFS on (l, r, k) is the correct solution. It has 27 * N^2 states, and edges are generated by looking at incoming edges to l and outgoing edges from r (for left and right steps), and then matching with the expected character. The BFS will correctly compute the shortest palindrome path from the middle to all (i, j). The distance to (i, j, 0) is the answer.

Now, we need to implement this BFS efficiently.
- State: (l, r, k) where l, r in 0..N-1, k in 0..26 (0 for no expected, 1..26 for 'a'..'z').
- We can map to an integer index: id = (l * N + r) * 27 + k.
- Precompute incoming edges: in_edges[v][c] = list of u such that u->v with label c.
- Precompute outgoing edges: out_edges[u][c] = list of v such that u->v with label c.
- BFS from all (m, m, 0) with distance 0. Use a deque.
- For each state (l, r, k) popped:
  - If k == 0:
    - For each c in 0..25:
      - For each u in in_edges[l][c]: (u, r, c) if not visited.
      - For each v in out_edges[r][c]: (l, v, c) if not visited.
  - If k > 0: let c = k-1.
    - For each u in in_edges[l][c]: (u, r, 0) if not visited.
    - For each v in out_edges[r][c]: (u, v, 0)? Wait, careful: the state is (l, r, k). The expected character is for the other side. If we are at (l, r, k) after a left step, we expect a right step with label c. So we should take a right step: for each v in out_edges[r][c], go to (l, v, 0). If we are at (l, r, k) after a right step, we expect a left step: for each u in in_edges[l][c], go to (u, r, 0). So both are valid. So we do both.
- The distance to (i, j, 0) is the answer.
- We also need to handle the case where the walk is just a single edge. That is, from (i, j, 0) we can go to (j, j, 0) with cost 1 if there is an edge i->j. In the BFS from the middle, this corresponds to: from (j, j, 0) we can go to (i, j, 0) with cost 1 if i->j. So we need to add transitions: from (m, m, 0) to (i, m, 0) if i->m, and to (m, i, 0) if i->m. These are exactly the "center" steps in the reverse direction? Actually, the forward center steps were: from (m, m, 0) we can take a left step to (m', m, c) and then a right step. The reverse of finishing a single edge is from (j, j, 0) to (i, j, 0) if i->j. So we can just add: from any (a, a, 0), for each edge a -> b, we can go to (a, b, 0) with cost 1? Wait, the edge is i->j. So from (j, j, 0) we go to (i, j, 0). So for a fixed middle a, if there is an edge b -> a, we can go to (b, a, 0). If there is an edge a -> b, we can go to (a, b, 0). So we add:
  - From (a, a, 0): for each b in out_edges[a][c] (i.e., a->b): (a, b, 0) with cost 1.
  - From (a, a, 0): for each b in in_edges[a][c] (i.e., b->a): (b, a, 0) with cost 1.
These are weight 1 edges. They will be processed from the deque's front.

We already have (0,0,0), (1,1,0), (2,2,0), (3,3,0) as sources.
From (0,0,0): out_edges[0]: (0,'a'), (1,'b'). So (0,0,0) -> (0,1,0) with cost 1? Wait, (a, a, 0) -> (a, b, 0) if a->b. So (0,0,0) -> (0,1,0) because 0->1 (b). Also in_edges[0]: (0,'a'), (3,'c'). So (0,0,0) -> (3,0,0) because 3->0 (c). Also from (0,0,0) with k=0, we can do left step: u->0 with 'a' gives (0,0,a); with 'c' gives (3,0,c). Right step: 0->v with 'a' gives (0,0,a); with 'b' gives (0,1,b).
So (0,1,0) and (3,0,0) get dist 1.
From (1,1,0): out: 1->1 (a), 1->2 (b) -> (1,1,0), (1,2,0) dist 1. in: 0->1 (b), 1->1 (a) -> (0,1,0), (1,1,0). So (1,2,0) dist 1.
From (2,2,0): out: 2->3 (a) -> (2,3,0) dist 1. in: 1->2 (b) -> (1,2,0) dist 1.
From (3,3,0): out: 3->0 (c) -> (3,0,0) dist 1 (already 1). in: 2->3 (a) -> (2,3,0) dist 1.
Now we have states with k != 0 at dist 1: (0,0,a), (3,0,c), (0,1,b), (1,1,a), (1,2,b), (2,3,b), (3,0,c) [again], etc.
Process (0,0,a): l=0, r=0, c='a'. Right step: 0->v with 'a' -> 0->0 (a) -> (0,0,0) visited. Left step: u->0 with 'a' -> 0->0 (a) -> (0,0,0) visited.

- States: (u, v)
- Edges:
  - (u, v) -> (u', v') weight 2 if u -> u' (c) and v' -> v (c).
  - (u, u) -> (u', u) weight 1 if u -> u' (c).
  - (u, v) -> (v, v) weight 1 if u -> v (c).
  - (u, v) -> (u, u) weight 1 if v -> u (c).
And we want the shortest path from (i, j) to any (m, m). The distance is the palindrome length.
We can run a multi-source BFS from all (m, m) in the reverse graph. The reverse graph edges are:
- (u', v') -> (u, v) weight 2 if u' -> u and v -> v'. (Note: this is the reverse of the first edge: forward u->u' and v'->v -> reverse u'->u and v->v')
- (u', u) -> (u, u) weight 1 if u' -> u.
- (v, v) -> (u, v) weight 1 if u -> v.
- (u, u) -> (u, v) weight 1 if v -> u.
This reverse graph has the same number of edges. We can run a 0-1-2 BFS from all (m, m) in this reverse graph. The distance to (i, j) is the answer. Since the graph is small, we can run Dijkstra with a binary heap. The number of nodes is N^2 = 10,000. The number of edges is at most 7 million. Dijkstra with binary heap on 10,000 nodes and 7 million edges might be slow in Python, but we can optimize. Actually, the number of edges is exactly the number of pairs of edges with the same label. Let's implement Dijkstra on the product graph with N^2 nodes and edges as described. We'll use a priority queue. The number of edges is at most N^2 * 26 * 26 = 6.76 million. In Python, storing 6.76 million edges in lists might use too much memory. We can generate edges on the fly. For each node (u, v) popped from the heap, we iterate over all letters c, and for each u' in out[u][c] and v' in in[v][c], we relax (u', v') with weight 2. Also, we relax the weight 1 edges: if u==v, for each u' in out[u][c], relax (u', u) with weight 1. Also, for each edge u->v (c), relax (v, v) with weight 1. For each edge v->u (c), relax (u, u) with weight 1. This on-the-fly generation avoids storing the product graph. The total number of edge relaxations is the same, but we don't store the edges. The time complexity: each node is popped once. For each node, we do O(26 * (out_degree + in_degree)) work. out_degree and in_degree are at most 26. So each node takes O(26*26) = O(676) time. For 10,000 nodes, that's 6.76 million operations. Each operation is a heap push. So 6.76 million heap pushes. In Python, 6.76 million heap pushes might be around 1-2 seconds. This is acceptable.

Let's write the code:
- N, matrix.
- out_edges[u] = [[] for _ in range(26)]; for each c, list of v.
- in_edges[v] = [[] for _ in range(26)]; for each c, list of u.
- dist = [[INF]*N for _ in range(N)]
- heap = []
- For m in 0..N-1: dist[m][m] = 0; push (0, m, m).
- While heap:
  - d, u, v = heappop
  - if d > dist[u][v]: continue
  - # weight 2 edges
  - for c in range(26):
    - for u' in out_edges[u][c]:
      - for v' in in_edges[v][c]:
        - nd = d + 2
        - if nd < dist[u'][v']:
          - dist[u'][v'] = nd
          - heappush(heap, (nd, u', v'))
  - # weight 1 edges
  - if u == v:
    - for c in range(26):
      - for u' in out_edges[u][c]:
        - nd = d + 1
        - if nd < dist[u'][u]:
          - dist[u'][u] = nd
          - heappush(heap, (nd, u', u))
  - # finish edges: u -> v
  - for c in range(26):
    - if v in out_edges[u][c]: # need a quick check
      - nd = d + 1
      - if nd < dist[v][v]:
        - dist[v][v] = nd
        - heappush(heap, (nd, v, v))
  - # finish edges: v -> u
  - for c in range(26):
    - if u in out_edges[v][c]:
      - nd = d + 1
      - if nd < dist[u][u]:
        - dist[u][u] = nd
        - heappush(heap, (nd, u, u))
But checking if v in out_edges[u][c] for every c is O(26). We can precompute a set of (v, c) for u. Or we can just iterate over the out_edges of u and check if v is the target. Since out_edges[u] is small (at most 26), we can do:
  - for (v_target, c) in out_edges[u]:
    - if v_target == v:
      - nd = d + 1
      - ...
  - for (u_target, c) in out_edges[v]:
    - if u_target == u:
      - ...
This is O(26) per node.

This Dijkstra will compute the shortest distance from any (m, m) to all (i, j). The answer for (i, j) is dist[i][j]. If dist[i][j] is INF, output -1.

Complexities:
- Nodes: N^2 = 10,000.
- Edges generated: for each node, for each c, out[u][c] * in[v][c]. Total ~ N^2 * 26^2 = 6.76M.
- Each edge relaxation: heap push/pop. O(log V) = O(log 10k) ~ 14.
- Total operations: ~ 6.76M * 14 = 95M. In Python, this might be borderline but with efficient heap and loops, it's possible. We can use array-based priority queue or optimize by not pushing if distance doesn't improve. We can also use a 0-1-2 BFS with a deque to avoid the log factor. Since weights are only 1 and 2, we can use a deque for 0-1 BFS? But weight 2 is not 0 or 1. We can use a deque with two levels: push weight 1 to front, weight 2 to back. But weight 2 is larger than weight 1, so it should go to the back. But if we have a deque, we need to ensure that we process nodes in order of increasing distance. A standard 0-1 BFS uses a deque and pushes weight 0 to front, weight 1 to back. For weight 2, we can just push to the back, but then weight 2 might be processed before some weight 1 nodes? Actually, if we push weight 1 to front and weight 2 to back, the deque will process weight 1 before weight 2, which is correct because weight 1 < weight 2. But we also have weight 2 edges that could be followed by weight 1 edges. The deque will process all weight 1 edges before any weight 2 edge from the same level. This is correct for 0-1 BFS. For 0-1-2 BFS, we can use a deque where we push weight 1 to the front, weight 2 to the back. But then weight 2 might be processed before some weight 1 edges that were discovered later? No, because weight 2 is larger, so any weight 1 edge discovered later will still have distance >= current distance + 1, which is less than current distance + 2 if the current distance is the same. Actually, if we have a node with distance d, its weight 1 neighbors have distance d+1, weight 2 neighbors have d+2. If we process the node, we push weight 1 neighbors to the front, weight 2 to the back. The front of the deque will have d+1 nodes, then the back will have d+2 nodes. This is exactly BFS order. This works as long as we don't have negative weights. So we can use a deque for 0-1-2 BFS! This eliminates the log factor and makes it O(V+E). That's much faster.

Let's implement 0-1-2 BFS with a deque:
- dist[u][v] initialized to INF.
- For all m, dist[m][m] = 0. Add to deque.
- While deque not empty:
  - pop left (u, v)
  - d = dist[u][v]
  - Process weight 1 edges:
    - if u == v: for each c, for each u' in out[u][c]: if d+1 < dist[u'][u]: dist[u'][u] = d+1; appendleft (u', u)
    - for each (v', c) in out[u]: if v' == v: if d+1 < dist[v][v]: ...; if d+1 < dist[u][u]? Wait, the finish edges: from (u, v) to (v, v) if u->v. So if there is an edge u->v (c): nd = d+1; if nd < dist[v][v]: dist[v][v] = nd; appendleft (v, v). Similarly for v->u: nd = d+1; if nd < dist[u][u]: dist[u][u] = nd; appendleft (u, u).
  - Process weight 2 edges:
    - for c in 0..25:
      - for u' in out[u][c]:
        - for v' in in[v][c]:
          - nd = d + 2
          - if nd < dist[u'][v']:
            - dist[u'][v'] = nd
            - append (u', v')  # to the back
This is O(V + E) with a small constant. The number of edge relaxations is the same ~6.76M, but without heap overhead. This should be very fast in Python.

Let's code this.</mm:think>### NOTES
The problem asks for the shortest walk from `i` to `j` whose edge labels form a palindrome.  
A standard solution builds a product graph on `N²` states `(u, v)` with edges:
* Both‑sides match: `(u, v) → (u', v')` weight 2 if there is an edge `u → u'` (c) and an edge `v' → v` (c) with the same label.  
* Center expansion (odd length): `(u, u) → (u', u)` weight 1 if `u → u'` (c).  
* Finish a single edge: `(u, v) → (v, v)` weight 1 if `u → v` (c), and symmetrically `(u, v) → (u, u)` weight 1 if `v → u` (c).  

The shortest distance from `(i, j)` to any `(m, m)` in this directed graph equals the palindrome length.  
Because `N ≤ 100`, `N² = 10,000`. The number of weight‑2 edges is at most `N² × 26 × 26 ≈ 6.8` million.  
We run a multi‑source 0‑1‑2 BFS from all `(m, m)` on the reverse graph. All edges have weight 1 or 2, so a deque gives `O(V+E)` ≈ 7 million operations – easily fast enough in Python.  

Key points:
* Precompute `out[u][c]` (destinations of `u` with label `c`) and `in[v][c]` (origins of `v` with label `c`).  
* 0‑1‑2 BFS: weight‑1 edges go to the front of the deque, weight‑2 edges to the back.  
* The answer for `(i, j)` is the distance to `(i, j)`; if unreachable, output `-1`.

## worker: Implement the solution in Python using a 0‑1‑2 BFS
The solution builds a product graph on `N²` states `(u, v)` and runs a 0‑1‑2 BFS from all diagonal states `(m, m)`.  
Edges:
* **Weight 2** – both‑sides match: `(u, v) → (u', v')` if `u → u'` and `v' → v` share a label.  
* **Weight 1** – center expansion (when `u == v`) and finishing a single edge (`u → v` or `v → u`).  

The BFS deque pushes weight‑1 edges to the front and weight‑2 edges to the back, guaranteeing nodes are processed in non‑decreasing distance order.  
The distance to `(i, j)` is the length of the shortest palindromic walk; unreachable pairs get `-1`.

## worker: Implement the 0-1-2 BFS on the product graph in Py
The solution constructs a product graph on `N²` states `(u, v)`.  
A 0‑1‑2 BFS is run from all diagonal states `(m, m)`. The deque processes weight‑1 edges at the front and weight‑2 edges at the back, guaranteeing distances are finalised in non‑decreasing order.  
* Weight‑2 edges correspond to matching the outermost remaining characters on both sides: `u → u'` and `v' → v` with the same label.  
* Weight‑1 edges handle the center (when `u == v`) and the trivial palindrome of length 1 (`u → v` or `v → u`).  
The answer for `(i, j)` is the BFS distance to state `(i, j)`; unreachable pairs output `-1`.  
Complexity: `O(N² × 26²)` ≈ 7·10⁶ edge relaxations, comfortably within limits for `N ≤ 100`.

## worker: Write the final Python code implementing the 0-1-2
The solution constructs a product graph on `N²` states `(u, v)` and runs a 0‑1‑2 BFS from all diagonal states `(m, m)`.  
* **Weight 2 edges** correspond to matching the outermost remaining characters: `u → u'` and `v' → v` with the same label.  
* **Weight 1 edges** handle the center (when `u == v`, take one step) and finishing a single edge (`u → v` or `v → u`).  

The BFS deque pushes weight‑1 edges to the front and weight‑2 edges to the back, guaranteeing nodes are processed in non‑decreasing distance order.  
The answer for `(i, j)` is the BFS distance to state `(i, j)`; unreachable pairs output `-1`.  
Complexity: `O(N² × 26²)` ≈ 7·10⁶ edge relaxations, comfortably within limits for `N ≤ 100`.

## worker: finalize
- **Product graph**: nodes are pairs `(u, v)`.  
- **0-1-2 BFS** from all diagonal states `(m, m)` finds shortest distances.  
- Weight‑2 edges pair an outgoing edge from `u` with an incoming edge to `v` having the same label.  
- Weight‑1 edges handle the center (`u == v`) and the trivial single‑edge palindrome (`u → v` or `v → u`).  
- Complexity: `O(N² × 26²)` ≈ 7·10⁶ operations for `N ≤ 100`, well within limits.  
- Memory: `O(N²)` for the distance matrix, edges are generated on the fly.
